I don’t really know. I’m trying to think if there were “more than one,” as you say, but I can’t separate things out neatly like that.

There have been times I’ve felt low, yes, but whether they were as low as this… I’m not sure. Life has its seasons, doesn’t it? Sometimes I look back and remember what was happening with the house or the weather more than how I actually felt. Like the winter the boiler kept cutting out — I remember sitting in my coat indoors, but I couldn’t tell you if I felt the same as I do now or just cold and fed up.

So… I can’t honestly say there’s been more than one episode like this. I just don’t know.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally scared me into coming is that I’ve been having these thoughts lately, like, “Maybe it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.” I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that, but it frightened me.\n\nI almost didn’t come in, honestly. I kept thinking, oh, I’m being dramatic, people my age have gloomy days, you know… my neighbor used to say aging is not for cowards. But this felt different enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It sort of started after my brother’s health changed so suddenly… and his behavior too. That threw me more than I expected. I remember around that time I kept telling myself I just needed to be practical, you know, make phone calls, keep track of things—my mother used to keep everything in little folders—but somewhere in there those thoughts started showing up. So… yes, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy activities or things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I used to love painting watercolors—nothing grand, just little landscapes, the way light hits a windowsill or a tree branch. I still take the paints out sometimes, line everything up like I’m going to do it, and then I just sit there looking at the blank paper.\n\nIt’s strange, because the colors are right there, but that little spark I used to get from them just… doesn’t come. Then I end up putting it all away. My husband used to tease me about getting blue paint on the tablecloth—well, ex-husband, I suppose—but now I can’t even seem to make a mess worth teasing about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. My skirts fit about the same, at least the ones with proper waistbands—though honestly half of what I wear now has elastic, which makes it harder to tell. My daughter once told me that elastic waistbands are the beginning of giving up, but I told her at my age comfort has earned its place.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, sleep has been difficult. I can fall asleep some nights, but not in any steady sort of way. It’s uneven, if that makes sense.\n\nThen I wake up around three or four in the morning, and my mind starts going over things—phone calls I need to make, forms, whether I said the right thing or handled something properly. It’s silly, because nothing is actually getting done at that hour. I just lie there thinking about it.\n\nBy morning I feel like I’ve hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything dramatic that way. I mean, I’m 68, so sometimes getting up from the low chair in the living room takes a bit more ceremony than it used to—but that chair was a terrible purchase anyway. My daughter said it looked “mid-century,” but I think that’s just code for uncomfortable.\n\nAs far as moving slowly… I suppose I have my slower mornings, but I haven’t thought of it as something especially different over the past months. It’s hard to separate what’s mood and what’s just… being a bit older, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Mostly about my brother. I keep going back over how he was declining and wondering why I didn’t see it sooner.\n\nI tell myself if I’d been a better sister—more attentive, more on top of things—maybe I could have noticed earlier and prevented some of it. Which, I know, sounds a bit grand, like I’m imagining I could control everything. My daughter would probably say I’m “rewriting the past,” she likes phrases like that from articles she reads online.\n\nBut it’s hard not to go over it. I think about little moments and wonder, “Was that a sign? Should I have done something then?” It sits with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you also lost interest in spending time with friends or family over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… though it shows up more with things I used to do on my own. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes — the brushes, the little tray, all of it — and then I just sit there looking at the paper.\n\nI used to love that part, deciding where the light would go, whether the blue needed more gray in it. There was a little art shop near where we lived years ago that had the most beautiful pigments… I remember buying a green I never even used properly.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there and the feeling just doesn’t come. The pleasure in the color and light, it just… doesn’t arrive. So I put everything away again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow more frequently over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more often lately, I think. That’s actually part of what frightened me enough to come here.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. But I’ll be lying there at night, or sometimes just sitting with a cup of tea gone cold — I do that constantly now, make tea and then forget it’s there — and the thought comes: maybe it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nAnd then I feel ashamed for even thinking it, because there are people who care about me. But yes, it’s been happening more than it used to, and it scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they have, in the sense that the thoughts themselves are what finally made me come here. I can still go through the motions, mostly, but it’s frightening to be sitting there and suddenly think, it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nI don’t have a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. It’s more like the thought comes in and then stays with me, and afterward I feel shaken. Sometimes I’ll try to distract myself — I’ll put the kettle on, or I’ll straighten some little pile of papers, which is ridiculous because I have piles everywhere — and then I realize I’m not really doing the thing, I’m just trying not to think.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting me. Maybe not in some dramatic outward way, but enough that it scared me into coming.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less enjoyment from being around other people, like friends or family, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: With people… I suppose a little, but the thing I notice most is when I’m alone with my paints.\n\nI still take out my watercolor things sometimes — the brushes, the tray, the paper. I’ll arrange them all, almost like if I set them out properly the feeling will come back. I used to get such pleasure from the color and the light, even just mixing a wash and seeing what it did.\n\nNow I just sit there staring at the paper. Nothing really comes. Then I feel foolish and pack it all away again.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're resting or moving more slowly than you used to, even when you're not feeling tired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I sit still a lot more than I used to, but that may not be the same thing you mean.\n\nSometimes I notice I’ve been standing in the kitchen for ages, holding something — a spoon, or the kettle lid — and not quite doing the next step. But then I think, well, I’ve always been a bit absentminded. My daughter used to tease me about that, even years ago, because I’d start one cupboard and end up reorganizing another.\n\nAs for actually moving slowly… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in the way someone else might notice. No one’s said anything to me about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less enjoyment from being around other people, like friends or family, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I know you asked about people, friends and family… and I suppose I’m a bit vague about that. What I keep thinking of is my watercolor set, actually.\n\nI still take it out sometimes — the little tray, the brushes, the paper — and I’ll set everything up as if I’m going to paint. I used to love that part, the colors and how the light changed things. But lately I just sit there staring at the blank paper, and then I end up putting it all away again. The pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period of feeling this low in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count that, really. I mean, I’ve had sad times, of course — everyone does by my age, don’t they? There were winters that felt a bit heavier, and times when the house seemed too quiet.\n\nBut “more than one period”... I don’t know. I tend to just get on with things and only later think, oh, perhaps that was a bad patch. My memory for that kind of thing isn’t very tidy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low before in your life, to the point where it significantly impacted your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that clearly. I’ve had difficult times before, certainly, but whether it was as low as this or whether it properly stopped me functioning… I’m not sure.\n\nI tend to think of things in bits, like, “Oh, that was the year I didn’t keep up with the garden,” or “that was when I stopped going to that book group,” but then I wonder if I’m making too much of it. And the garden has always had a mind of its own anyway — once the bindweed gets going, you know, it’s like it’s determined to win.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t say for certain that I’ve felt this low before in a way that really affected everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember, anyway. I mean, I’ve had doctors ask how I’m coping, especially as you get older and they hand you all those little forms with boxes to tick — I never know quite how honest to be with those.\n\nBut an actual diagnosis… I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying that, no. Although my memory for appointments is a bit muddled sometimes; I keep old letters in a drawer and then can’t find the one I need. So I’d have to say I’m not sure, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode like this before, where you felt similarly low and it lasted for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to compare it to other times, but it all gets a bit blurred.\n\nThere were stretches when I wasn’t myself, I suppose, but whether they were like this and went on for a long time… I couldn’t say with confidence. Sometimes I only realize something was bad after it’s passed, like when you look back and notice you haven’t done the little ordinary things. I do that with old photographs too — I’ll think, “Oh, that was before we changed the curtains,” instead of remembering how I felt.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a clear episode before that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode in your life where you felt as low as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’m trying to think if there were “more than one,” as you say, but I can’t separate things out neatly like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve felt low, yes, but whether they were as low as this… I’m not sure. Life has its seasons, doesn’t it? Sometimes I look back and remember what was happening with the house or the weather more than how I actually felt. Like the winter the boiler kept cutting out — I remember sitting in my coat indoors, but I couldn’t tell you if I felt the same as I do now or just cold and fed up.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say there’s been more than one episode like this. I just don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.1", "F33.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most days I feel as if a gray film has settled over everything, and even when I’m making tea or looking out at my garden, there’s this quiet heaviness in me that I can’t quite reason my way out of.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, but I’ll sit there staring at the paper and put it all away because the pleasure I used to feel in color and light just doesn’t seem to arrive.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: At night I fall asleep unevenly and then wake around three or four thinking about phone calls, forms, and whether I handled things correctly, and by morning I feel as if I’ve hardly rested.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep going back over my brother’s decline and telling myself that if I had been a better sister, or more attentive, I would have noticed sooner and prevented some of this.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: What scared me enough to come in is that I’ve started having thoughts like, ‘It would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow,’ even though I don’t have a plan to harm myself.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still take care of the essentials, but I’ve pulled back from my walking group and mentoring because I can’t seem to follow through or be fully present with people the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about eight months, since my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember, anyway. I mean, I’ve had doctors ask how I’m coping, especially as you get older and they hand you all those little forms with boxes to tick — I never know quite how honest to be with those.\n\nBut an actual diagnosis… I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying that, no. Although my memory for appointments is a bit muddled sometimes; I keep old letters in a drawer and then can’t find the one I need. Sometimes I only realize something was bad after it’s passed, like when you look back and notice you haven’t done the little ordinary things. I do that with old photographs too — I’ll think, “Oh, that was before we changed the curtains,” instead of remembering how I felt.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a clear episode before that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode in your life where you felt as low as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’m trying to think if there were “more than one,” as you say, but I can’t separate things out neatly like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve felt low, yes, but whether they were as low as this… I’m not sure. Life has its seasons, doesn’t it? Sometimes I look back and remember what was happening with the house or the weather more than how I actually felt. Like the winter the boiler kept cutting out — I remember sitting in my coat indoors, but I couldn’t tell you if I felt the same as I do now or just cold and fed up.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say there’s been more than one episode like this. I just don’t know.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt as low as you do now for an extended period at any other time in your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 19\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question asks about whether there were previous extended episodes of similarly low mood at other times in life, but the provided symptom profile only covers the current episode and does not include past episode history or recurrence."}

I don’t know, really. I’ve had low patches, but I’m not sure I could say they were this low or went on in the same way.

It’s funny, I remember little things around those times more than the feeling itself — like what room I was in, or whether I’d been keeping up with letters, or even what was on the kitchen counter. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally scared me into coming is that I’ve been having these thoughts lately, like, “Maybe it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.” I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that, but it frightened me.\n\nI almost didn’t come in, honestly. I kept thinking, oh, I’m being dramatic, people my age have gloomy days, you know… my neighbor used to say aging is not for cowards. But this felt different enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It sort of started after my brother’s health changed so suddenly… and his behavior too. That threw me more than I expected. I remember around that time I kept telling myself I just needed to be practical, you know, make phone calls, keep track of things—my mother used to keep everything in little folders—but somewhere in there those thoughts started showing up. So… yes, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy activities or things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I used to love painting watercolors—nothing grand, just little landscapes, the way light hits a windowsill or a tree branch. I still take the paints out sometimes, line everything up like I’m going to do it, and then I just sit there looking at the blank paper.\n\nIt’s strange, because the colors are right there, but that little spark I used to get from them just… doesn’t come. Then I end up putting it all away. My husband used to tease me about getting blue paint on the tablecloth—well, ex-husband, I suppose—but now I can’t even seem to make a mess worth teasing about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. My skirts fit about the same, at least the ones with proper waistbands—though honestly half of what I wear now has elastic, which makes it harder to tell. My daughter once told me that elastic waistbands are the beginning of giving up, but I told her at my age comfort has earned its place.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, sleep has been difficult. I can fall asleep some nights, but not in any steady sort of way. It’s uneven, if that makes sense.\n\nThen I wake up around three or four in the morning, and my mind starts going over things—phone calls I need to make, forms, whether I said the right thing or handled something properly. It’s silly, because nothing is actually getting done at that hour. I just lie there thinking about it.\n\nBy morning I feel like I’ve hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything dramatic that way. I mean, I’m 68, so sometimes getting up from the low chair in the living room takes a bit more ceremony than it used to—but that chair was a terrible purchase anyway. My daughter said it looked “mid-century,” but I think that’s just code for uncomfortable.\n\nAs far as moving slowly… I suppose I have my slower mornings, but I haven’t thought of it as something especially different over the past months. It’s hard to separate what’s mood and what’s just… being a bit older, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Mostly about my brother. I keep going back over how he was declining and wondering why I didn’t see it sooner.\n\nI tell myself if I’d been a better sister—more attentive, more on top of things—maybe I could have noticed earlier and prevented some of it. Which, I know, sounds a bit grand, like I’m imagining I could control everything. My daughter would probably say I’m “rewriting the past,” she likes phrases like that from articles she reads online.\n\nBut it’s hard not to go over it. I think about little moments and wonder, “Was that a sign? Should I have done something then?” It sits with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you also lost interest in spending time with friends or family over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… though it shows up more with things I used to do on my own. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes — the brushes, the little tray, all of it — and then I just sit there looking at the paper.\n\nI used to love that part, deciding where the light would go, whether the blue needed more gray in it. There was a little art shop near where we lived years ago that had the most beautiful pigments… I remember buying a green I never even used properly.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there and the feeling just doesn’t come. The pleasure in the color and light, it just… doesn’t arrive. So I put everything away again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow more frequently over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yes… more often lately, I think. That’s actually part of what frightened me enough to come here.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. But I’ll be lying there at night, or sometimes just sitting with a cup of tea gone cold — I do that constantly now, make tea and then forget it’s there — and the thought comes: maybe it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nAnd then I feel ashamed for even thinking it, because there are people who care about me. But yes, it’s been happening more than it used to, and it scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they have, in the sense that the thoughts themselves are what finally made me come here. I can still go through the motions, mostly, but it’s frightening to be sitting there and suddenly think, it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nI don’t have a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. It’s more like the thought comes in and then stays with me, and afterward I feel shaken. Sometimes I’ll try to distract myself — I’ll put the kettle on, or I’ll straighten some little pile of papers, which is ridiculous because I have piles everywhere — and then I realize I’m not really doing the thing, I’m just trying not to think.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting me. Maybe not in some dramatic outward way, but enough that it scared me into coming.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less enjoyment from being around other people, like friends or family, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: With people… I suppose a little, but the thing I notice most is when I’m alone with my paints.\n\nI still take out my watercolor things sometimes — the brushes, the tray, the paper. I’ll arrange them all, almost like if I set them out properly the feeling will come back. I used to get such pleasure from the color and the light, even just mixing a wash and seeing what it did.\n\nNow I just sit there staring at the paper. Nothing really comes. Then I feel foolish and pack it all away again.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're resting or moving more slowly than you used to, even when you're not feeling tired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I sit still a lot more than I used to, but that may not be the same thing you mean.\n\nSometimes I notice I’ve been standing in the kitchen for ages, holding something — a spoon, or the kettle lid — and not quite doing the next step. But then I think, well, I’ve always been a bit absentminded. My daughter used to tease me about that, even years ago, because I’d start one cupboard and end up reorganizing another.\n\nAs for actually moving slowly… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in the way someone else might notice. No one’s said anything to me about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less enjoyment from being around other people, like friends or family, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I know you asked about people, friends and family… and I suppose I’m a bit vague about that. What I keep thinking of is my watercolor set, actually.\n\nI still take it out sometimes — the little tray, the brushes, the paper — and I’ll set everything up as if I’m going to paint. I used to love that part, the colors and how the light changed things. But lately I just sit there staring at the blank paper, and then I end up putting it all away again. The pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period of feeling this low in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count that, really. I mean, I’ve had sad times, of course — everyone does by my age, don’t they? There were winters that felt a bit heavier, and times when the house seemed too quiet.\n\nBut “more than one period”... I don’t know. I tend to just get on with things and only later think, oh, perhaps that was a bad patch. My memory for that kind of thing isn’t very tidy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low before in your life, to the point where it significantly impacted your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that clearly. I’ve had difficult times before, certainly, but whether it was as low as this or whether it properly stopped me functioning… I’m not sure.\n\nI tend to think of things in bits, like, “Oh, that was the year I didn’t keep up with the garden,” or “that was when I stopped going to that book group,” but then I wonder if I’m making too much of it. And the garden has always had a mind of its own anyway — once the bindweed gets going, you know, it’s like it’s determined to win.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t say for certain that I’ve felt this low before in a way that really affected everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember, anyway. I mean, I’ve had doctors ask how I’m coping, especially as you get older and they hand you all those little forms with boxes to tick — I never know quite how honest to be with those.\n\nBut an actual diagnosis… I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying that, no. Although my memory for appointments is a bit muddled sometimes; I keep old letters in a drawer and then can’t find the one I need. So I’d have to say I’m not sure, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode like this before, where you felt similarly low and it lasted for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to compare it to other times, but it all gets a bit blurred.\n\nThere were stretches when I wasn’t myself, I suppose, but whether they were like this and went on for a long time… I couldn’t say with confidence. Sometimes I only realize something was bad after it’s passed, like when you look back and notice you haven’t done the little ordinary things. I do that with old photographs too — I’ll think, “Oh, that was before we changed the curtains,” instead of remembering how I felt.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a clear episode before that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode in your life where you felt as low as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’m trying to think if there were “more than one,” as you say, but I can’t separate things out neatly like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve felt low, yes, but whether they were as low as this… I’m not sure. Life has its seasons, doesn’t it? Sometimes I look back and remember what was happening with the house or the weather more than how I actually felt. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as low as you do now for an extended period at any other time in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, really. I’ve had low patches, but I’m not sure I could say they were this low or went on in the same way.\n\nIt’s funny, I remember little things around those times more than the feeling itself — like what room I was in, or whether I’d been keeping up with letters, or even what was on the kitchen counter. But pinning down, “Yes, that was the same as now and it lasted a long time”… I don’t think I can say that clearly. So I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What I keep thinking of is my watercolor set, actually.\n\nI still take it out sometimes — the little tray, the brushes, the paper — and I’ll set everything up as if I’m going to paint. I used to love that part, the colors and how the light changed things. But lately I just sit there staring at the blank paper, and then I end up putting it all away again. The pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period of feeling this low in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count that, really. I mean, I’ve had sad times, of course — everyone does by my age, don’t they? There were winters that felt a bit heavier, and times when the house seemed too quiet.\n\nBut “more than one period”... I don’t know. I tend to just get on with things and only later think, oh, perhaps that was a bad patch. My memory for that kind of thing isn’t very tidy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low before in your life, to the point where it significantly impacted your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that clearly. I’ve had difficult times before, certainly, but whether it was as low as this or whether it properly stopped me functioning… I’m not sure.\n\nI tend to think of things in bits, like, “Oh, that was the year I didn’t keep up with the garden,” or “that was when I stopped going to that book group,” but then I wonder if I’m making too much of it. And the garden has always had a mind of its own anyway — once the bindweed gets going, you know, it’s like it’s determined to win.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t say for certain that I’ve felt this low before in a way that really affected everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember, anyway. I mean, I’ve had doctors ask how I’m coping, especially as you get older and they hand you all those little forms with boxes to tick — I never know quite how honest to be with those.\n\nBut an actual diagnosis… I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying that, no. Although my memory for appointments is a bit muddled sometimes; I keep old letters in a drawer and then can’t find the one I need. So I’d have to say I’m not sure, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode like this before, where you felt similarly low and it lasted for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to compare it to other times, but it all gets a bit blurred.\n\nThere were stretches when I wasn’t myself, I suppose, but whether they were like this and went on for a long time… I couldn’t say with confidence. Sometimes I only realize something was bad after it’s passed, like when you look back and notice you haven’t done the little ordinary things. I do that with old photographs too — I’ll think, “Oh, that was before we changed the curtains,” instead of remembering how I felt.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a clear episode before that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode in your life where you felt as low as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’m trying to think if there were “more than one,” as you say, but I can’t separate things out neatly like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve felt low, yes, but whether they were as low as this… I’m not sure. Life has its seasons, doesn’t it? Sometimes I look back and remember what was happening with the house or the weather more than how I actually felt. Like the winter the boiler kept cutting out — I remember sitting in my coat indoors, but I couldn’t tell you if I felt the same as I do now or just cold and fed up.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say there’s been more than one episode like this. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as low as you do now for an extended period at any other time in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, really. I’ve had low patches, but I’m not sure I could say they were this low or went on in the same way.\n\nIt’s funny, I remember little things around those times more than the feeling itself — like what room I was in, or whether I’d been keeping up with letters, or even what was on the kitchen counter. But pinning down, “Yes, that was the same as now and it lasted a long time”… I don’t think I can say that clearly. So I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally scared me into coming is that I’ve been having these thoughts lately, like, “Maybe it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.” I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that, but it frightened me.\n\nI almost didn’t come in, honestly. I kept thinking, oh, I’m being dramatic, people my age have gloomy days, you know… my neighbor used to say aging is not for cowards. But this felt different enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It sort of started after my brother’s health changed so suddenly… and his behavior too. That threw me more than I expected. I remember around that time I kept telling myself I just needed to be practical, you know, make phone calls, keep track of things—my mother used to keep everything in little folders—but somewhere in there those thoughts started showing up. I used to love painting watercolors—nothing grand, just little landscapes, the way light hits a windowsill or a tree branch. But yes, it’s been happening more than it used to, and it scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they have, in the sense that the thoughts themselves are what finally made me come here. Maybe not in some dramatic outward way, but enough that it scared me into coming.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less enjoyment from being around other people, like friends or family, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: With people… I suppose a little, but the thing I notice most is when I’m alone with my paints.\n\nI still take out my watercolor things sometimes — the brushes, the tray, the paper. I’ll arrange them all, almost like if I set them out properly the feeling will come back. I used to get such pleasure from the color and the light, even just mixing a wash and seeing what it did.\n\nNow I just sit there staring at the paper. Nothing really comes.
